I knew I would have less time for sprint work than in Palafrugell. I still tried to make time each morning to talk with a few people about ideas I had been thinking about.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “At the end of our first lunch at Lido Aurora, on the beach in Pescara, on 23 September.” (from left to right: Jacob R., Juliana, Marco, Caleb, Justin, Alex, Paolo, Lily, Tim, Annabelle, Jekaterina, Žan, Matthias, Becky, Mark, Shai, Sigal, Carlton, Moreno, Valentino, Simon, Daniele, Lilian, Sam)On the first morning, I spoke with Daniele Procida about documentation and AI. I was curious about how good documentation like Django’s fits with the way we use AI tools today, and whether it could already do some of the work of a skill. It was a short conversation, but I enjoyed hearing Daniele’s ideas and seeing an approach to generating a text version of the documentation that I would like to explore further.The next morning, I found time to talk with Simon Charette and return to the idea of an ORM compatibility matrix. I had started a prototype with Jacob Walls in Palafrugell, and a year later I was talking with someone again to work out what to do next. Simon stressed the value of generating a matrix from the database feature flags automatically and suggested using Python’s descriptor protocol. I already knew how valuable his feedback could be, and it helped to ask him a question, explain what I was unsure about and think it through together.Around us, other people were working on the ORM, the frontend, documentation, Django’s async support and plenty of other topics. People with different levels of experience sat at the same tables, where they could ask for help or a review. I enjoyed seeing people who had been listening at first grow more confident and join the discussions.The mid-morning coffee break, funded by Cuttlesoft, was in the next room. We could get up, have as many freshly made espressos or cappuccinos as we wanted, get something good to eat and talk without having to find a café for the whole group. We had first thought about having breakfast together, but the coffee break fit the rhythm of the three mornings well.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “The coffee break at Hotel Plaza on 25 September, the last morning of sprints.”One thing I never managed to do in Pescara in 2026 was share photos, moments and impressions on my personal Mastodon account as the days went by, as I normally do. This time I took as many photos as I could and wrote a few posts on the official Django on the Med account. Unfortunately, I never got around to posting about the event on my own account, during the sprints or afterwards.Alessia joined us on the second day to take photos and record video. I had asked her to help because I had been free to document everything in Palafrugell, and in Pescara I knew I would have other things to look after. That morning we recorded a few short video interviews with people who wanted to share where they came from and why they were there. I was glad we could keep a record of people’s voices and faces as well as having the photos.The Costa dei TrabocchiFor the afternoon of the first day, I had suggested a bike ride along the Costa dei Trabocchi coast. After lunch on the beach, we walked to the station together to take the regional train to the coastal village of San Vito Chietino. I thought we had plenty of time, but settling the last bills at the restaurant and waiting at the ticket machines meant we still had to hurry a little.«[…] Django on the Med was my first face-to-face encounter with other Djangonauts. I knew the Django community is a friendly place, but my expectations were exceeded.[…] During the lovely afternoon excursions, we were able to connect […] I feel truly inspired and empowered […] I hope that other contributors can have a similar experience […]»As soon as we arrived, everyone bought a return ticket. We had just seen how much a simple thing like that could slow us down, and the small station had only one ticket machine.We picked up the bikes from the shop I had contacted well in advance and headed towards the Adriatic cycle path. Caleb had brought his Brompton folding bike and Johannes his Penny skateboard. The shop owner had warned me that two old rails still crossed the entrance to the path. We needed to get off our bikes to cross them because the wheels could slip. I stopped there to remind the group and wait for the last few people to cross them.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Heading south along the Adriatic cycle path on the Costa dei Trabocchi on the afternoon of 23 September.” (from left to right: Annabelle, Juliana, Jacob W., Jekaterina, Alex, Charlie, Sam)When I looked up, nearly everyone was already cycling the wrong way. Daniele and Valentino had set off north at full speed, and the others had followed, assuming they knew where to go. Caleb went after them and brought them back. I had tried to think of all the little things that might go wrong. The whole group riding away in the opposite direction had never crossed my mind.Once we were all together again, we headed south along the old railway route. The path goes through tunnels and stretches that open out onto the sea. We could ride next to each other, talk and move between groups as we went. I stopped to explain what the trabocchi are and how these wooden fishing structures gave the whole coast its name.On the Punta Isolata headland, part of the group stopped at the kiosk opposite the trabocco of the same name. A few of us went a little further. We took off our shoes on the beach and put our feet in the water. The sea was a little rough, but the summer sun had kept it pleasantly warm. Some people took photos, others watched the waves, and some preferred a drink at the kiosk. I got to show people places I knew well and enjoy them with the people I usually talk to about Django.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “With Annabelle, Sam and Juliana at the water’s edge at Punta Isolata on the afternoon of 23 September.” (from left to right: Paolo, Annabelle, Juliana, Sam)On the way back I reminded everyone that they could make their own plans for dinner. While we waited on the platform at San Vito station, we started forming groups based on what we felt like eating: vegetarian or vegan food, meat or fish. I joined the group that wanted fish. We found a small room for nine at La Barcaccia, a very good seafood restaurant in central Pescara, where we ate local fish and seafood dishes. After spending the afternoon together, it worked well to carry on the evening in smaller groups.«Django on the Med in Pescara was truly amazing. Being able to interact with Django first line contributors in person was truly an unreal experience.I’ve been working remotely for more than 13 years and it still amazes me how refreshing can be to meet fellow developers in person. In the context of advancing a web framework like Django, these events acquire more value than any other means of interaction.»From the hills to the mountainsFor the afternoon of the second day, I also wanted to show people the countryside inland from Pescara. I had thought of several possibilities before I knew which ones we could actually arrange. Mark Smith helped me start the conversation with JetBrains, where he works, and their support made it possible for us to travel together. We would leave Pescara at sea level, cross the countryside, then head up into the hills and finally into the mountains.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “At the Pescara bus station on the second day, waiting for the bus for the tour supported by JetBrains.” (from left to right: Alessia, Juliana, Alex, Matthias, Jacob W., Johannes)With the private bus, we could travel together and return straight to the city centre at the end of the evening.Our first stop was La Tenuta del Priore, a winery in Collecorvino, in the countryside near Pescara. The vineyards were near the Tavo River valley, about 150 metres above sea level. It was harvest time, so we saw the vineyards and the winery while everyone was busy with the harvest, then sat down on the patio for the tasting. In front of us were the hills, the vineyards and the mountains we would head towards later.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Tasting wine and local food on the patio at La Tenuta del Priore in Collecorvino on 24 September.” (in the foreground, from left to right: Mark, Jacob W., Justin, Simon, Alex, Matthias, Lily, Lilian, Annabelle, Carlton, Paolo)I already knew the people at the winery, which helped me arrange the afternoon. I could explain what kind of event it was, who was coming from abroad and what different needs we had to consider. Being able to talk to people in advance and adjust the plans together made a difference throughout the preparations.«You’d be working on a problem in the morning with someone else, and perhaps by the end of the sprint that morning you’d be thinking:“Maybe we need to freshen our brains, think about something else, and then maybe you’ll be able to solve the problem when you get back to tackling it.”And then when you do have that moment a few hours later, you’ll still be with the person you are working on the problem with.»I wanted everyone to be able to enjoy the experience. We had arranged an alternative for people who did not drink wine. For dinner, we wanted to serve only traditional dishes from the area. Most local dishes are based on meat, so we worked with the restaurant to find vegetarian and vegan options that were also part of the local tradition. This was one of the things that needed the most attention. It took some planning before anyone sat down to order.After the tasting we stopped in Penne, a medieval town on a high hill in the province of Pescara. Vanda, the local guide I had hired at the last minute, took us through Penne’s narrow streets, churches and viewpoints and told us about them in English. I already knew the town, and I still learned something new about its history.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “During the guided walk through the medieval streets of Penne on 24 September.”We stopped for a photo at the highest point in town, about 450 metres above sea level. We could see across the valley and the countryside below, all the way to the sea. Behind the town, the sun was setting over the Gran Sasso massif.Then we headed up towards Villa Celiera, a small mountain village on the slopes of Gran Sasso. We had dinner at La Locanda del Voltigno in a tiny hamlet just over 900 metres above sea level. We were in the Voltigno plateau area, inside the Gran Sasso and Monti della Laga National Park. Cristiano, the owner and chef, had set up a long table in a room for our group. We had worked together on alternatives for the menu, including ciabotto, a traditional vegetable dish. After the first courses, we went outside to see how arrosticini, the local meat skewers, are cooked. Cristiano explained how to cook them on the furnacella, a long, narrow charcoal grill shaped like a gutter, and I translated.Standing around the furnacella, talking and eating the arrosticini while they were still hot from the charcoal was one of the most authentic experiences I could share. I was glad to do it with friends who had come from so many different places. Eventually we went back inside, finished dinner and stayed to talk longer than planned, thanks to a patient bus driver.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the evening of 24 September at La Locanda del Voltigno in Villa Celiera, Cristiano showed us how to cook arrosticini over charcoal, and I translated for the group.” (from left to right: Lilian, Justin, Mark, Jacob R., Paolo, Cristiano)It was already late when we got back on the bus. The lightning over the sea and the conversations that were still going on brought a very full day to a close. With JetBrains’ support, I had been able to arrange much more than the three mornings of sprints.«It’s three serious days. We got 25 people together, and we work solidly in the mornings. The afternoons are more social, and you do carry on the discussions: on the bus coming back from the restaurants, there are people talking about nested transactions and all sorts of things going on.»I was pleased, and I also knew how much work it had taken. I had set aside a week off for the event and kept dealing with bookings, attendance, menus and calls until the last moment. With a room in central Pescara, people could have made their own plans for most afternoons and dinners. I chose to arrange those extra activities because I wanted to share as much of the area as I could with people who had come from far away. When I look back on this edition, I want to remember both what we got from it and how much time it took to make it happen.The last photoOn the last morning, 25 September, the seafront was still wet from the storm we had seen coming in from the sea on the bus ride back. I went for another walk before heading to the hotel. After the long day and a night of rain, some people took the chance to sleep a little longer, so the morning started more slowly. Andrew Northall joined us for the last morning, the only one he could attend.By then people knew their way around the city. They knew the room, had tasks in progress and had sat next to different people over the three days. I had a chance to talk with Carlton about how things had gone and what to do next with Django on the Med.«[…] I’m here because, besides the fun that I’m having here, being in the sprints for a few days charges me and inspires my work on Django for months to come.»We started thinking about the next edition on Gozo, a Maltese island in the middle of the Mediterranean, with help from Joseph Zammit on the island. Joseph had planned everything for Pescara, but had to pull out shortly before the sprints began. Sitting together with Carlton again made that conversation quick too. The year before, we had taken a photo in Dublin to remember our decision to try organizing the first event. This time we took another while Carlton wrote down ideas for the next edition in his notebook.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the morning of 25 September, Carlton and I had just decided to meet again on Gozo, Malta, in October 2027 for the third edition of Django on the Med.” (from left to right: Paolo, Carlton)That morning, with the activities from the previous days behind me, I finally got to sit down at my computer. I opened a proposal for an ORM compatibility matrix based on feature flags to write down and share the work from 2025 and the ideas Simon and I had discussed the day before.We stopped at one in the afternoon, half an hour earlier than on the previous days, to give people time to catch their trains or flights without rushing. We took the Django banner outside for our usual group photo in front of the hotel. Then came the goodbyes and hugs. Those who had time stayed for one last quick lunch at Pesciolillo, a small restaurant in the city centre. We tried to make sure the people leaving soon could order first. The station and everyone’s accommodation were close by, so we could spend a bit more time together and everyone could still get on their way easily.After the night of rain, the sun came back swiftly and it was even warmer. We ate outside, then those of us still around went for a gelato. By then I was exhausted and very pleased with how it had gone.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Our usual gelato selfie after lunch on Friday 25 September, the last moment together before we said goodbye.” (from left to right: Paolo, Mark, Becky, Jekaterina, Jacob W., Charlie, Sam, Jacob R., Andrew)On each of the three mornings I had managed to do at least one of the things I had planned for the sprints. I talked with Daniele about documentation and AI, discussed the ORM feature flags matrix with Simon and planned the next edition with Carlton. As an organizer, I often felt there was one more thing to do before I could stop. Looking back, I got a lot out of those short conversations too.«Django on the Med 2026 in Pescara is a wrap! 🇮🇹We filed issues, opened pull requests, reviewed code, cycled many kilometres, explored Abruzzo, ate a lot of good food and shared quite a few glasses of wine. We worked, talked, laughed and had a great time together. 🍷Here we all are in our final group photo. Thank you to everyone who made these three days special. 🤗See you in Malta in the 2nd week of October 2027, with Joseph Zammit as our local point person! 🇲🇹»When I got home late that afternoon, I collapsed on the sofa. From the messages and photos in the chat, I could see that some people still in Pescara had gone to the beach and others were making plans for dinner together. I was glad they were still enjoying their time together while I could stop thinking about what came next.«Home, sweet home, but I am missing Pescara already and all the exceptional people that made Django on the Med special.Unmeasurable thanks to Paolo Melchiorre and his fellow locals for keeping us always active, heavenly fed, well caffeinated, incredibly happy and slightly tipsy.What a blast ❤️‍🔥»The last photo I have from those days is of me and Carlton near Pescara’s historic railway station, a few metres from the sprint venue. We are laughing while we wait for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport. We still had time to talk a little about how it had gone and say goodbye with a hug. After so many trips to see each other elsewhere, I was glad I had been able to show him a little of home.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “My last photo of the event, a short walk from Hotel Plaza, while Carlton and I waited for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport.” (from left to right: Carlton, Paolo).

On the morning of 23 September, the first day of the sprints, my wife dropped me off in Pescara on her way to work. Along the seafront there were palms, a few clouds and the sun rising over the Adriatic. I took a short walk on the beach and took a few photos, which felt like a good way to start the day.At the hotel I put the Django banner by the entrance and checked the room. Then I went to a nearby pastry shop, where we had agreed to meet anyone whose accommodation did not include breakfast. I ordered a cappuccino and a bomba alla crema, a doughnut filled with custard, and waited for Jacob and Alex to join me for breakfast.At half past eight we were ready to start. I welcomed everyone, thanked the participants and sponsors, went over the practical details and handed over to Carlton. As in the first edition, he led a half-hour brainstorming session and took notes on large sheets of paper. We talked about what we liked about Django, what problems we saw and what we wanted to work on. Small groups then formed naturally around shared interests.«When Paolo wasn’t otherwise showing us around Abruzzo last week at Django on the Med 🏖️, I had the pleasure of supporting a number of groups there […]Special thanks to Annabelle and Simon for taking up my suggestion to pair on some delicate challenges around NULL handling in the ORM. […] the three of us now have more context for tackling whatever comes next in this area.»I kept moving from one table to another while also looking after the day’s arrangements. I had to contact the bike shop about rentals for the afternoon, arrange lunch for the whole group and check that everything was ready for the coffee break. I knew I would have less time for sprint work than in Palafrugell. Joseph had planned everything for Pescara, but had to pull out shortly before the sprints began. Sitting together with Carlton again made that conversation quick too. The year before, we had taken a photo in Dublin to remember our decision to try organizing the first event. This time we took another while Carlton wrote down ideas for the next edition in his notebook.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the morning of 25 September, Carlton and I had just decided to meet again on Gozo, Malta, in October 2027 for the third edition of Django on the Med.” (from left to right: Paolo, Carlton)That morning, with the activities from the previous days behind me, I finally got to sit down at my computer. I opened a proposal for an ORM compatibility matrix based on feature flags to write down and share the work from 2025 and the ideas Simon and I had discussed the day before.We stopped at one in the afternoon, half an hour earlier than on the previous days, to give people time to catch their trains or flights without rushing. We took the Django banner outside for our usual group photo in front of the hotel. Then came the goodbyes and hugs. Those who had time stayed for one last quick lunch at Pesciolillo, a small restaurant in the city centre. We tried to make sure the people leaving soon could order first. The station and everyone’s accommodation were close by, so we could spend a bit more time together and everyone could still get on their way easily.After the night of rain, the sun came back quickly and it was even warmer. We ate outside, then those of us still around went for a gelato. By then I was exhausted and very pleased with how it had gone.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Our usual gelato selfie after lunch on Friday 25 September, the last moment together before we remarked goodbye.” (from left to right: Paolo, Mark, Becky, Jekaterina, Jacob W., Charlie, Sam, Jacob R., Andrew)On each of the three mornings I had managed to do at least one of the things I had planned for the sprints. I talked with Daniele about documentation and AI, discussed the ORM feature flags matrix with Simon and planned the next edition with Carlton. As an organizer, I often felt there was one more thing to do before I could stop. Looking back, I got a lot out of those short conversations too.«Django on the Med 2026 in Pescara is a wrap! 🇮🇹We filed issues, opened pull requests, reviewed code, cycled many kilometres, explored Abruzzo, ate a lot of good food and shared quite a few glasses of wine. We worked, talked, laughed and had a great time together. 🍷Here we all are in our final group photo. Thank you to everyone who made these three days special. 🤗See you in Malta in the 2nd week of October 2027, with Joseph Zammit as our local point person! 🇲🇹»When I got home late that afternoon, I collapsed on the sofa. From the messages and photos in the chat, I could see that some people still in Pescara had gone to the beach and others were making plans for dinner together. I was glad they were still enjoying their time together while I could stop thinking about what came next.«Home, sweet home, but I am missing Pescara already and all the exceptional people that made Django on the Med special.Unmeasurable thanks to Paolo Melchiorre and his fellow locals for keeping us always active, heavenly fed, well caffeinated, incredibly happy and slightly tipsy.What a blast ❤️‍🔥»The last photo I have from those days is of me and Carlton near Pescara’s historic railway station, a few metres from the sprint venue. We are laughing while we wait for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport. We still had time to talk a little about how it had gone and say goodbye with a hug. After so many trips to see each other elsewhere, I was glad I had been able to show him a little of home.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “My last photo of the event, a short walk from Hotel Plaza, while Carlton and I waited for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport.” (from left to right: Carlton, Paolo).

At the hotel I put the Django banner by the entrance and checked the room. Then I went to a nearby pastry shop, where we had agreed to meet anyone whose accommodation did not include breakfast. I ordered a cappuccino and a bomba alla crema, a doughnut filled with custard, and waited for Jacob and Alex to join me for breakfast.At half past eight we were ready to start. I welcomed everyone, thanked the participants and sponsors, went over the practical details and handed over to Carlton. As in the first edition, he led a half-hour brainstorming session and took notes on large sheets of paper. We talked about what we liked about Django, what problems we saw and what we wanted to work on. Small groups then formed naturally around shared interests.«When Paolo wasn’t otherwise showing us around Abruzzo last week at Django on the Med 🏖️, I had the pleasure of supporting a number of groups there […]Special thanks to Annabelle and Simon for taking up my suggestion to pair on some delicate challenges around NULL handling in the ORM. […] the three of us now have more context for tackling whatever comes next in this area.»I kept moving from one table to another while also looking after the day’s arrangements. I had to contact the bike shop about rentals for the afternoon, arrange lunch for the whole group and check that everything was ready for the coffee break. I knew I would have less time for sprint work than in Palafrugell. I still tried to make time each morning to talk with a few people about ideas I had been thinking about.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “At the end of our first lunch at Lido Aurora, on the beach in Pescara, on 23 September.” (from left to right: Jacob R., Juliana, Marco, Caleb, Justin, Alex, Paolo, Lily, Tim, Annabelle, Jekaterina, Žan, Matthias, Becky, Mark, Shai, Sigal, Carlton, Moreno, Valentino, Simon, Daniele, Lilian, Sam)On the first morning, I spoke with Daniele Procida about documentation and AI. I was curious about how good documentation like Django’s fits with the way we use AI tools today, and whether it could already do some of the work of a skill. It was a short conversation, but I enjoyed hearing Daniele’s ideas and seeing an approach to generating a text version of the documentation that I would like to explore further.The next morning, I found time to talk with Simon Charette and return to the idea of an ORM compatibility matrix. I had started a prototype with Jacob Walls in Palafrugell, and a year later I was talking with someone again to work out what to do next. Simon stressed the value of generating a matrix from the database feature flags automatically and suggested using Python’s descriptor protocol. I already knew how valuable his feedback could be, and it helped to ask him a question, explain what I was unsure about and think it through together.Around us, other people were working on the ORM, the frontend, documentation, Django’s async support and plenty of other topics. People with different levels of experience sat at the same tables, where they could ask for help or a review. I enjoyed seeing people who had been listening at first grow more confident and join the discussions.The mid-morning coffee break, funded by Cuttlesoft, was in the next room. We could get up, have as many freshly made espressos or cappuccinos as we wanted, get something good to eat and talk without having to find a café for the whole group. We had first thought about having breakfast together, but the coffee break fit the rhythm of the three mornings well.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “The coffee break at Hotel Plaza on 25 September, the last morning of sprints.”One thing I never managed to do in Pescara in 2026 was share photos, moments and impressions on my personal Mastodon account as the days went by, as I normally do. This time I took as many photos as I could and wrote a few posts on the official Django on the Med account. Unfortunately, I never got around to posting about the event on my own account, during the sprints or afterwards.Alessia joined us on the second day to take photos and record video. I had asked her to help because I had been free to document everything in Palafrugell, and in Pescara I knew I would have other things to look after. That morning we recorded a few short video interviews with people who wanted to share where they came from and why they were there. I was glad we could keep a record of people’s voices and faces as well as having the photos.The Costa dei TrabocchiFor the afternoon of the first day, I had suggested a bike ride along the Costa dei Trabocchi coast. After lunch on the beach, we walked to the station together to take the regional train to the coastal village of San Vito Chietino. I thought we had plenty of time, but settling the last bills at the restaurant and waiting at the ticket machines meant we still had to hurry a little.«[…] Django on the Med was my first face-to-face encounter with other Djangonauts. I knew the Django community is a friendly place, but my expectations were exceeded.[…] During the lovely afternoon excursions, we were able to connect […] I feel truly inspired and empowered […] I hope that other contributors can have a similar experience […]»As soon as we arrived, everyone bought a return ticket. We had just seen how much a simple thing like that could slow us down, and the small station had only one ticket machine.We picked up the bikes from the shop I had contacted well in advance and headed towards the Adriatic cycle path. Caleb had brought his Brompton folding bike and Johannes his Penny skateboard. The shop owner had warned me that two old rails still crossed the entrance to the path. We needed to get off our bikes to cross them because the wheels could slip. I stopped there to remind the group and wait for the last few people to cross them.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Heading south along the Adriatic cycle path on the Costa dei Trabocchi on the afternoon of 23 September.” (from left to right: Annabelle, Juliana, Jacob W., Jekaterina, Alex, Charlie, Sam)When I looked up, nearly everyone was already cycling the wrong way. Daniele and Valentino had set off north at full speed, and the others had followed, assuming they knew where to go. Caleb went after them and brought them back. I had tried to think of all the little things that might go wrong. The whole group riding away in the opposite direction had never crossed my mind.Once we were all together again, we headed south along the old railway route. The path goes through tunnels and stretches that open out onto the sea. We could ride next to each other, talk and move between groups as we went. I stopped to explain what the trabocchi are and how these wooden fishing structures gave the whole coast its name.On the Punta Isolata headland, part of the group stopped at the kiosk opposite the trabocco of the same name. A few of us went a little further. We took off our shoes on the beach and put our feet in the water. The sea was a little rough, but the summer sun had kept it pleasantly warm. Some people took photos, others watched the waves, and some preferred a drink at the kiosk. I got to show people places I knew well and enjoy them with the people I usually talk to about Django.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “With Annabelle, Sam and Juliana at the water’s edge at Punta Isolata on the afternoon of 23 September.” (from left to right: Paolo, Annabelle, Juliana, Sam)On the way back I reminded everyone that they could make their own plans for dinner. While we waited on the platform at San Vito station, we started forming groups based on what we felt like eating: vegetarian or vegan food, meat or fish. I joined the group that wanted fish. We found a small room for nine at La Barcaccia, a very good seafood restaurant in central Pescara, where we ate local fish and seafood dishes. After spending the afternoon together, it worked well to carry on the evening in smaller groups.«Django on the Med in Pescara was truly amazing. Being able to interact with Django first line contributors in person was truly an unreal experience.I’ve been working remotely for more than 13 years and it still amazes me how refreshing can be to meet fellow developers in person. In the context of advancing a web framework like Django, these events acquire more value than any other means of interaction.»From the hills to the mountainsFor the afternoon of the second day, I also wanted to show people the countryside inland from Pescara. I had thought of several possibilities before I knew which ones we could actually arrange. Mark Smith helped me start the conversation with JetBrains, where he works, and their support made it possible for us to travel together. We would leave Pescara at sea level, cross the countryside, then head up into the hills and finally into the mountains.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “At the Pescara bus station on the second day, waiting for the bus for the tour supported by JetBrains.” (from left to right: Alessia, Juliana, Alex, Matthias, Jacob W., Johannes)With the private bus, we could travel together and return straight to the city centre at the end of the evening.Our first stop was La Tenuta del Priore, a winery in Collecorvino, in the countryside near Pescara. The vineyards were near the Tavo River valley, about 150 metres above sea level. It was harvest time, so we saw the vineyards and the winery while everyone was busy with the harvest, then sat down on the patio for the tasting. In front of us were the hills, the vineyards and the mountains we would head towards later.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Tasting wine and local food on the patio at La Tenuta del Priore in Collecorvino on 24 September.” (in the foreground, from left to right: Mark, Jacob W., Justin, Simon, Alex, Matthias, Lily, Lilian, Annabelle, Carlton, Paolo)I already knew the people at the winery, which helped me arrange the afternoon. I could explain what kind of event it was, who was coming from abroad and what different needs we had to consider. Being able to talk to people in advance and adjust the plans together made a difference throughout the preparations.«You’d be working on a problem in the morning with someone else, and perhaps by the end of the sprint that morning you’d be thinking:“Maybe we need to freshen our brains, think about something else, and then maybe you’ll be able to solve the problem when you get back to tackling it.”And then when you do have that moment a few hours later, you’ll still be with the person you are working on the problem with.»I wanted everyone to be able to enjoy the experience. We had arranged an alternative for people who did not drink wine. For dinner, we wanted to serve only traditional dishes from the area. Most local dishes are based on meat, so we worked with the restaurant to find vegetarian and vegan options that were also part of the local tradition. This was one of the things that needed the most attention. It took some planning before anyone sat down to order.After the tasting we stopped in Penne, a medieval town on a high hill in the province of Pescara. Vanda, the local guide I had hired at the last minute, took us through Penne’s narrow streets, churches and viewpoints and told us about them in English. I already knew the town, and I still learned something fresh about its history.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “During the guided walk through the medieval streets of Penne on 24 September.”We stopped for a photo at the highest point in town, about 450 metres above sea level. We could see across the valley and the countryside below, all the way to the sea. Behind the town, the sun was setting over the Gran Sasso massif.Then we headed up towards Villa Celiera, a small mountain village on the slopes of Gran Sasso. We had dinner at La Locanda del Voltigno in a tiny hamlet just over 900 metres above sea level. We were in the Voltigno plateau area, inside the Gran Sasso and Monti della Laga National Park. Cristiano, the owner and chef, had set up a long table in a room for our group. We had worked together on alternatives for the menu, including ciabotto, a traditional vegetable dish. After the first courses, we went outside to see how arrosticini, the local meat skewers, are cooked. Cristiano explained how to cook them on the furnacella, a long, narrow charcoal grill shaped like a gutter, and I translated.Standing around the furnacella, talking and eating the arrosticini while they were still hot from the charcoal was one of the most authentic experiences I could share. I was glad to do it with friends who had come from so many different places. Eventually we went back inside, finished dinner and stayed to talk longer than planned, thanks to a patient bus driver.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the evening of 24 September at La Locanda del Voltigno in Villa Celiera, Cristiano revealed us how to cook arrosticini over charcoal, and I translated for the group.” (from left to right: Lilian, Justin, Mark, Jacob R., Paolo, Cristiano)It was already late when we got back on the bus. The lightning over the sea and the conversations that were still going on brought a very full day to a close. With JetBrains’ support, I had been able to arrange much more than the three mornings of sprints.«It’s three serious days. We got 25 people together, and we work solidly in the mornings. The afternoons are more social, and you do carry on the discussions: on the bus coming back from the restaurants, there are people talking about nested transactions and all sorts of things going on.»I was pleased, and I also knew how much work it had taken. I had set aside a week off for the event and kept dealing with bookings, attendance, menus and calls until the last moment. With a room in central Pescara, people could have made their own plans for most afternoons and dinners. I chose to arrange those extra activities because I wanted to share as much of the area as I could with people who had come from far away. When I look back on this edition, I want to remember both what we got from it and how much time it took to make it happen.The last photoOn the last morning, 25 September, the seafront was still wet from the storm we had seen coming in from the sea on the bus ride back. I went for another walk before heading to the hotel. After the long day and a night of rain, some people took the chance to sleep a little longer, so the morning started more slowly. Andrew Northall joined us for the last morning, the only one he could attend.By then people knew their way around the city. They knew the room, had tasks in progress and had sat next to different people over the three days. I had a chance to talk with Carlton about how things had gone and what to do next with Django on the Med.«[…] I’m here because, besides the fun that I’m having here, being in the sprints for a few days charges me and inspires my work on Django for months to come.»We started thinking about the next edition on Gozo, a Maltese island in the middle of the Mediterranean, with help from Joseph Zammit on the island. Joseph had planned everything for Pescara, but had to pull out shortly before the sprints began. Sitting together with Carlton again made that conversation quick too. The year before, we had taken a photo in Dublin to remember our decision to try organizing the first event. This time we took another while Carlton wrote down ideas for the next edition in his notebook.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the morning of 25 September, Carlton and I had just decided to meet again on Gozo, Malta, in October 2027 for the third edition of Django on the Med.” (from left to right: Paolo, Carlton)That morning, with the activities from the previous days behind me, I finally got to sit down at my computer. I opened a proposal for an ORM compatibility matrix based on feature flags to write down and share the work from 2025 and the ideas Simon and I had discussed the day before.We stopped at one in the afternoon, half an hour earlier than on the previous days, to give people time to catch their trains or flights without rushing. We took the Django banner outside for our usual group photo in front of the hotel. Then came the goodbyes and hugs. Those who had time stayed for one last quick lunch at Pesciolillo, a small restaurant in the city centre. We tried to make sure the people leaving soon could order first. The station and everyone’s accommodation were close by, so we could spend a bit more time together and everyone could still get on their way easily.After the night of rain, the sun came back promptly and it was even warmer. We ate outside, then those of us still around went for a gelato. By then I was exhausted and very pleased with how it had gone.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Our usual gelato selfie after lunch on Friday 25 September, the last moment together before we said goodbye.” (from left to right: Paolo, Mark, Becky, Jekaterina, Jacob W., Charlie, Sam, Jacob R., Andrew)On each of the three mornings I had managed to do at least one of the things I had planned for the sprints. Looking back, I got a lot out of those short conversations too.«Django on the Med 2026 in Pescara is a wrap! 🇮🇹We filed issues, opened pull requests, reviewed code, cycled many kilometres, explored Abruzzo, ate a lot of good food and shared quite a few glasses of wine. We had arranged an alternative for people who did not drink wine. For dinner, we wanted to serve only traditional dishes from the area. Most local dishes are based on meat, so we worked with the restaurant to find vegetarian and vegan options that were also part of the local tradition. This was one of the things that needed the most attention. It took some planning before anyone sat down to order.After the tasting we stopped in Penne, a medieval town on a high hill in the province of Pescara. Vanda, the local guide I had hired at the last minute, took us through Penne’s narrow streets, churches and viewpoints and told us about them in English. I already knew the town, and I still learned something new about its history.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “During the guided walk through the medieval streets of Penne on 24 September.”We stopped for a photo at the highest point in town, about 450 metres above sea level. We could see across the valley and the countryside below, all the way to the sea. Behind the town, the sun was setting over the Gran Sasso massif.Then we headed up towards Villa Celiera, a small mountain village on the slopes of Gran Sasso. We had dinner at La Locanda del Voltigno in a tiny hamlet just over 900 metres above sea level. We were in the Voltigno plateau area, inside the Gran Sasso and Monti della Laga National Park. Cristiano, the owner and chef, had set up a long table in a room for our group. We had worked together on alternatives for the menu, including ciabotto, a traditional vegetable dish. After the first courses, we went outside to see how arrosticini, the local meat skewers, are cooked. Cristiano explained how to cook them on the furnacella, a long, narrow charcoal grill shaped like a gutter, and I translated.Standing around the furnacella, talking and eating the arrosticini while they were still hot from the charcoal was one of the most authentic experiences I could share. I was glad to do it with friends who had come from so many different places. Eventually we went back inside, finished dinner and stayed to talk longer than planned, thanks to a patient bus driver.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the evening of 24 September at La Locanda del Voltigno in Villa Celiera, Cristiano showed us how to cook arrosticini over charcoal, and I translated for the group.” (from left to right: Lilian, Justin, Mark, Jacob R., Paolo, Cristiano)It was already late when we got back on the bus. The lightning over the sea and the conversations that were still going on brought a very full day to a close. With JetBrains’ support, I had been able to arrange much more than the three mornings of sprints.«It’s three serious days. We got 25 people together, and we work solidly in the mornings. The afternoons are more social, and you do carry on the discussions: on the bus coming back from the restaurants, there are people talking about nested transactions and all sorts of things going on.»I was pleased, and I also knew how much work it had taken. I had set aside a week off for the event and kept dealing with bookings, attendance, menus and calls until the last moment. With a room in central Pescara, people could have made their own plans for most afternoons and dinners. I chose to arrange those extra activities because I wanted to share as much of the area as I could with people who had come from far away. When I look back on this edition, I want to remember both what we got from it and how much time it took to make it happen.The last photoOn the last morning, 25 September, the seafront was still wet from the storm we had seen coming in from the sea on the bus ride back. I went for another walk before heading to the hotel. After the long day and a night of rain, some people took the chance to sleep a little longer, so the morning started more slowly. Andrew Northall joined us for the last morning, the only one he could attend.By then people knew their way around the city. They knew the room, had tasks in progress and had sat next to different people over the three days. I had a chance to talk with Carlton about how things had gone and what to do next with Django on the Med.«[…] I’m here because, besides the fun that I’m having here, being in the sprints for a few days charges me and inspires my work on Django for months to come.»We started thinking about the next edition on Gozo, a Maltese island in the middle of the Mediterranean, with help from Joseph Zammit on the island. Joseph had planned everything for Pescara, but had to pull out shortly before the sprints began. Sitting together with Carlton again made that conversation quick too. The year before, we had taken a photo in Dublin to remember our decision to try organizing the first event. This time we took another while Carlton wrote down ideas for the next edition in his notebook.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the morning of 25 September, Carlton and I had just decided to meet again on Gozo, Malta, in October 2027 for the third edition of Django on the Med.” (from left to right: Paolo, Carlton)That morning, with the activities from the previous days behind me, I finally got to sit down at my computer. I opened a proposal for an ORM compatibility matrix based on feature flags to write down and share the work from 2025 and the ideas Simon and I had discussed the day before.We stopped at one in the afternoon, half an hour earlier than on the previous days, to give people time to catch their trains or flights without rushing. We took the Django banner outside for our usual group photo in front of the hotel. Then came the goodbyes and hugs. Those who had time stayed for one last quick lunch at Pesciolillo, a small restaurant in the city centre. We tried to make sure the people leaving soon could order first. The station and everyone’s accommodation were close by, so we could spend a bit more time together and everyone could still get on their way easily.After the night of rain, the sun came back swiftly and it was even warmer. We ate outside, then those of us still around went for a gelato. By then I was exhausted and very pleased with how it had gone.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Our usual gelato selfie after lunch on Friday 25 September, the last moment together before we remarked goodbye.” (from left to right: Paolo, Mark, Becky, Jekaterina, Jacob W., Charlie, Sam, Jacob R., Andrew)On each of the three mornings I had managed to do at least one of the things I had planned for the sprints. I talked with Daniele about documentation and AI, discussed the ORM feature flags matrix with Simon and planned the next edition with Carlton. As an organizer, I often felt there was one more thing to do before I could stop. Looking back, I got a lot out of those short conversations too.«Django on the Med 2026 in Pescara is a wrap! 🇮🇹We filed issues, opened pull requests, reviewed code, cycled many kilometres, explored Abruzzo, ate a lot of good food and shared quite a few glasses of wine. We worked, talked, laughed and had a great time together. 🍷Here we all are in our final group photo. Thank you to everyone who made these three days special. 🤗See you in Malta in the 2nd week of October 2027, with Joseph Zammit as our local point person! 🇲🇹»When I got home late that afternoon, I collapsed on the sofa. From the messages and photos in the chat, I could see that some people still in Pescara had gone to the beach and others were making plans for dinner together. I was glad they were still enjoying their time together while I could stop thinking about what came next.«Home, sweet home, but I am missing Pescara already and all the exceptional people that made Django on the Med special.Unmeasurable thanks to Paolo Melchiorre and his fellow locals for keeping us always active, heavenly fed, well caffeinated, incredibly happy and slightly tipsy.What a blast ❤️‍🔥»The last photo I have from those days is of me and Carlton near Pescara’s historic railway station, a few metres from the sprint venue. We are laughing while we wait for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport. We still had time to talk a little about how it had gone and say goodbye with a hug. After so many trips to see each other elsewhere, I was glad I had been able to show him a little of home.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “My last photo of the event, a short walk from Hotel Plaza, while Carlton and I waited for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport.” (from left to right: Carlton, Paolo).

In a recent development, I kept moving from one table to another while also looking after the day’s arrangements. I had to contact the bike shop about rentals for the afternoon, arrange lunch for the whole group and check that everything was ready for the coffee break. I knew I would have less time for sprint work than in Palafrugell. I still tried to make time each morning to talk with a few people about ideas I had been thinking about.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “At the end of our first lunch at Lido Aurora, on the beach in Pescara, on 23 September.” (from left to right: Jacob R., Juliana, Marco, Caleb, Justin, Alex, Paolo, Lily, Tim, Annabelle, Jekaterina, Žan, Matthias, Becky, Mark, Shai, Sigal, Carlton, Moreno, Valentino, Simon, Daniele, Lilian, Sam)On the first morning, I spoke with Daniele Procida about documentation and AI. I was curious about how good documentation like Django’s fits with the way we use AI tools today, and whether it could already do some of the work of a skill. It was a short conversation, but I enjoyed hearing Daniele’s ideas and seeing an approach to generating a text version of the documentation that I would like to explore further.The next morning, I found time to talk with Simon Charette and return to the idea of an ORM compatibility matrix. I had started a prototype with Jacob Walls in Palafrugell, and a year later I was talking with someone again to work out what to do next. Simon stressed the value of generating a matrix from the database feature flags automatically and suggested using Python’s descriptor protocol. I already knew how valuable his feedback could be, and it helped to ask him a question, explain what I was unsure about and think it through together.Around us, other people were working on the ORM, the frontend, documentation, Django’s async support and plenty of other topics. People with different levels of experience sat at the same tables, where they could ask for help or a review. I enjoyed seeing people who had been listening at first grow more confident and join the discussions.The mid-morning coffee break, funded by Cuttlesoft, was in the next room. We could get up, have as many freshly made espressos or cappuccinos as we wanted, get something good to eat and talk without having to find a café for the whole group. We had first thought about having breakfast together, but the coffee break fit the rhythm of the three mornings well.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “The coffee break at Hotel Plaza on 25 September, the last morning of sprints.”One thing I never managed to do in Pescara in 2026 was share photos, moments and impressions on my personal Mastodon account as the days went by, as I normally do. This time I took as many photos as I could and wrote a few posts on the official Django on the Med account. Unfortunately, I never got around to posting about the event on my own account, during the sprints or afterwards.Alessia joined us on the second day to take photos and record video. I had asked her to help because I had been free to document everything in Palafrugell, and in Pescara I knew I would have other things to look after. That morning we recorded a few short video interviews with people who wanted to share where they came from and why they were there. I was glad we could keep a record of people’s voices and faces as well as having the photos.The Costa dei TrabocchiFor the afternoon of the first day, I had suggested a bike ride along the Costa dei Trabocchi coast. After lunch on the beach, we walked to the station together to take the regional train to the coastal village of San Vito Chietino. I thought we had plenty of time, but settling the last bills at the restaurant and waiting at the ticket machines meant we still had to hurry a little.«[…] Django on the Med was my first face-to-face encounter with other Djangonauts. I knew the Django community is a friendly place, but my expectations were exceeded.[…] During the lovely afternoon excursions, we were able to connect […] I feel truly inspired and empowered […] I hope that other contributors can have a similar experience […]»As soon as we arrived, everyone bought a return ticket. We had just seen how much a simple thing like that could slow us down, and the small station had only one ticket machine.We picked up the bikes from the shop I had contacted well in advance and headed towards the Adriatic cycle path. Caleb had brought his Brompton folding bike and Johannes his Penny skateboard. The shop owner had warned me that two old rails still crossed the entrance to the path. We needed to get off our bikes to cross them because the wheels could slip. I stopped there to remind the group and wait for the last few people to cross them.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Heading south along the Adriatic cycle path on the Costa dei Trabocchi on the afternoon of 23 September.” (from left to right: Annabelle, Juliana, Jacob W., Jekaterina, Alex, Charlie, Sam)When I looked up, nearly everyone was already cycling the wrong way. Daniele and Valentino had set off north at full speed, and the others had followed, assuming they knew where to go. Caleb went after them and brought them back. I had tried to think of all the little things that might go wrong. The whole group riding away in the opposite direction had never crossed my mind.Once we were all together again, we headed south along the old railway route. The path goes through tunnels and stretches that open out onto the sea. We could ride next to each other, talk and move between groups as we went. I stopped to explain what the trabocchi are and how these wooden fishing structures gave the whole coast its name.On the Punta Isolata headland, part of the group stopped at the kiosk opposite the trabocco of the same name. A few of us went a little further. We took off our shoes on the beach and put our feet in the water. The sea was a little rough, but the summer sun had kept it pleasantly warm. Some people took photos, others watched the waves, and some preferred a drink at the kiosk. I got to show people places I knew well and enjoy them with the people I usually talk to about Django.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “With Annabelle, Sam and Juliana at the water’s edge at Punta Isolata on the afternoon of 23 September.” (from left to right: Paolo, Annabelle, Juliana, Sam)On the way back I reminded everyone that they could make their own looks for dinner. While we waited on the platform at San Vito station, we started forming groups based on what we felt like eating: vegetarian or vegan food, meat or fish. I joined the group that wanted fish. We found a small room for nine at La Barcaccia, a very good seafood restaurant in central Pescara, where we ate local fish and seafood dishes. After spending the afternoon together, it worked well to carry on the evening in smaller groups.«Django on the Med in Pescara was truly amazing. Being able to interact with Django first line contributors in person was truly an unreal experience.I’ve been working remotely for more than 13 years and it still amazes me how refreshing can be to meet fellow developers in person. In the context of advancing a web framework like Django, these events acquire more value than any other means of interaction.»From the hills to the mountainsFor the afternoon of the second day, I also wanted to show people the countryside inland from Pescara. I had thought of several possibilities before I knew which ones we could actually arrange. Mark Smith helped me start the conversation with JetBrains, where he works, and their support made it possible for us to travel together. We would leave Pescara at sea level, cross the countryside, then head up into the hills and finally into the mountains.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “At the Pescara bus station on the second day, waiting for the bus for the tour supported by JetBrains.” (from left to right: Alessia, Juliana, Alex, Matthias, Jacob W., Johannes)With the private bus, we could travel together and return straight to the city centre at the end of the evening.Our first stop was La Tenuta del Priore, a winery in Collecorvino, in the countryside near Pescara. The vineyards were near the Tavo River valley, about 150 metres above sea level. It was harvest time, so we saw the vineyards and the winery while everyone was busy with the harvest, then sat down on the patio for the tasting. In front of us were the hills, the vineyards and the mountains we would head towards later.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Tasting wine and local food on the patio at La Tenuta del Priore in Collecorvino on 24 September.” (in the foreground, from left to right: Mark, Jacob W., Justin, Simon, Alex, Matthias, Lily, Lilian, Annabelle, Carlton, Paolo)I already knew the people at the winery, which helped me arrange the afternoon. I could explain what kind of event it was, who was coming from abroad and what different needs we had to consider. Being able to talk to people in advance and adjust the plans together made a difference throughout the preparations.«You’d be working on a problem in the morning with someone else, and perhaps by the end of the sprint that morning you’d be thinking:“Maybe we need to freshen our brains, think about something else, and then maybe you’ll be able to solve the problem when you get back to tackling it.”And then when you do have that moment a few hours later, you’ll still be with the person you are working on the problem with.»I wanted everyone to be able to enjoy the experience. We had arranged an alternative for people who did not drink wine. For dinner, we wanted to serve only traditional dishes from the area. I had thought of several possibilities before I knew which ones we could actually arrange. Mark Smith helped me start the conversation with JetBrains, where he works, and their support made it possible for us to travel together. We would leave Pescara at sea level, cross the countryside, then head up into the hills and finally into the mountains.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “At the Pescara bus station on the second day, waiting for the bus for the tour supported by JetBrains.” (from left to right: Alessia, Juliana, Alex, Matthias, Jacob W., Johannes)With the private bus, we could travel together and return straight to the city centre at the end of the evening.Our first stop was La Tenuta del Priore, a winery in Collecorvino, in the countryside near Pescara. The vineyards were near the Tavo River valley, about 150 metres above sea level. It was harvest time, so we saw the vineyards and the winery while everyone was busy with the harvest, then sat down on the patio for the tasting. In front of us were the hills, the vineyards and the mountains we would head towards later.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Tasting wine and local food on the patio at La Tenuta del Priore in Collecorvino on 24 September.” (in the foreground, from left to right: Mark, Jacob W., Justin, Simon, Alex, Matthias, Lily, Lilian, Annabelle, Carlton, Paolo)I already knew the people at the winery, which helped me arrange the afternoon. I could explain what kind of event it was, who was coming from abroad and what different needs we had to consider. Being able to talk to people in advance and adjust the plans together made a difference throughout the preparations.«You’d be working on a problem in the morning with someone else, and perhaps by the end of the sprint that morning you’d be thinking:“Maybe we need to freshen our brains, think about something else, and then maybe you’ll be able to solve the problem when you get back to tackling it.”And then when you do have that moment a few hours later, you’ll still be with the person you are working on the problem with.»I wanted everyone to be able to enjoy the experience. We had arranged an alternative for people who did not drink wine. For dinner, we wanted to serve only traditional dishes from the area. Most local dishes are based on meat, so we worked with the restaurant to find vegetarian and vegan options that were also part of the local tradition. This was one of the things that needed the most attention. It took some planning before anyone sat down to order.After the tasting we stopped in Penne, a medieval town on a high hill in the province of Pescara. Vanda, the local guide I had hired at the last minute, took us through Penne’s narrow streets, churches and viewpoints and told us about them in English. I already knew the town, and I still learned something new about its history.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “During the guided walk through the medieval streets of Penne on 24 September.”We stopped for a photo at the highest point in town, about 450 metres above sea level. We could see across the valley and the countryside below, all the way to the sea. Behind the town, the sun was setting over the Gran Sasso massif.Then we headed up towards Villa Celiera, a small mountain village on the slopes of Gran Sasso. We had dinner at La Locanda del Voltigno in a tiny hamlet just over 900 metres above sea level. We were in the Voltigno plateau area, inside the Gran Sasso and Monti della Laga National Park. Cristiano, the owner and chef, had set up a long table in a room for our group. We had worked together on alternatives for the menu, including ciabotto, a traditional vegetable dish. After the first courses, we went outside to see how arrosticini, the local meat skewers, are cooked. Cristiano explained how to cook them on the furnacella, a long, narrow charcoal grill shaped like a gutter, and I translated.Standing around the furnacella, talking and eating the arrosticini while they were still hot from the charcoal was one of the most authentic experiences I could share. I was glad to do it with friends who had come from so many different places. Eventually we went back inside, finished dinner and stayed to talk longer than planned, thanks to a patient bus driver.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the evening of 24 September at La Locanda del Voltigno in Villa Celiera, Cristiano showed us how to cook arrosticini over charcoal, and I translated for the group.” (from left to right: Lilian, Justin, Mark, Jacob R., Paolo, Cristiano)It was already late when we got back on the bus. The lightning over the sea and the conversations that were still going on brought a very full day to a close. With JetBrains’ support, I had been able to arrange much more than the three mornings of sprints.«It’s three serious days. We got 25 people together, and we work solidly in the mornings. The afternoons are more social, and you do carry on the discussions: on the bus coming back from the restaurants, there are people talking about nested transactions and all sorts of things going on.»I was pleased, and I also knew how much work it had taken. I had set aside a week off for the event and kept dealing with bookings, attendance, menus and calls until the last moment. With a room in central Pescara, people could have made their own plans for most afternoons and dinners. I chose to arrange those extra activities because I wanted to share as much of the area as I could with people who had come from far away. When I look back on this edition, I want to remember both what we got from it and how much time it took to make it happen.The last photoOn the last morning, 25 September, the seafront was still wet from the storm we had seen coming in from the sea on the bus ride back. I went for another walk before heading to the hotel. After the long day and a night of rain, some people took the chance to sleep a little longer, so the morning started more slowly. Andrew Northall joined us for the last morning, the only one he could attend.By then people knew their way around the city. They knew the room, had tasks in progress and had sat next to different people over the three days. I had a chance to talk with Carlton about how things had gone and what to do next with Django on the Med.«[…] I’m here because, besides the fun that I’m having here, being in the sprints for a few days charges me and inspires my work on Django for months to come.»We started thinking about the next edition on Gozo, a Maltese island in the middle of the Mediterranean, with help from Joseph Zammit on the island. Joseph had planned everything for Pescara, but had to pull out shortly before the sprints began. Sitting together with Carlton again made that conversation quick too. The year before, we had taken a photo in Dublin to remember our decision to try organizing the first event. This time we took another while Carlton wrote down ideas for the next edition in his notebook.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the morning of 25 September, Carlton and I had just decided to meet again on Gozo, Malta, in October 2027 for the third edition of Django on the Med.” (from left to right: Paolo, Carlton)That morning, with the activities from the previous days behind me, I finally got to sit down at my computer. I opened a proposal for an ORM compatibility matrix based on feature flags to write down and share the work from 2025 and the ideas Simon and I had discussed the day before.We stopped at one in the afternoon, half an hour earlier than on the previous days, to give people time to catch their trains or flights without rushing. We took the Django banner outside for our usual group photo in front of the hotel. Then came the goodbyes and hugs. Those who had time stayed for one last quick lunch at Pesciolillo, a small restaurant in the city centre. We tried to make sure the people leaving soon could order first. The station and everyone’s accommodation were close by, so we could spend a bit more time together and everyone could still get on their way easily.After the night of rain, the sun came back rapidly and it was even warmer. We ate outside, then those of us still around went for a gelato. By then I was exhausted and very pleased with how it had gone.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Our usual gelato selfie after lunch on Friday 25 September, the last moment together before we stated goodbye.” (from left to right: Paolo, Mark, Becky, Jekaterina, Jacob W., Charlie, Sam, Jacob R., Andrew)On each of the three mornings I had managed to do at least one of the things I had planned for the sprints. I talked with Daniele about documentation and AI, discussed the ORM feature flags matrix with Simon and planned the next edition with Carlton. As an organizer, I often felt there was one more thing to do before I could stop. Looking back, I got a lot out of those short conversations too.«Django on the Med 2026 in Pescara is a wrap! 🇮🇹We filed concerns, opened pull requests, reviewed code, cycled many kilometres, explored Abruzzo, ate a lot of good food and shared quite a few glasses of wine. We worked, talked, laughed and had a great time together. 🍷Here we all are in our final group photo. Thank you to everyone who made these three days special. 🤗See you in Malta in the 2nd week of October 2027, with Joseph Zammit as our local point person! 🇲🇹»When I got home late that afternoon, I collapsed on the sofa. From the messages and photos in the chat, I could see that some people still in Pescara had gone to the beach and others were making plans for dinner together. I was glad they were still enjoying their time together while I could stop thinking about what came next.«Home, sweet home, but I am missing Pescara already and all the exceptional people that made Django on the Med special.Unmeasurable thanks to Paolo Melchiorre and his fellow locals for keeping us always active, heavenly fed, well caffeinated, incredibly happy and slightly tipsy.What a blast ❤️‍🔥»The last photo I have from those days is of me and Carlton near Pescara’s historic railway station, a few metres from the sprint venue. We are laughing while we wait for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport. We still had time to talk a little about how it had gone and say goodbye with a hug. After so many trips to see each other elsewhere, I was glad I had been able to show him a little of home.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “My last photo of the event, a short walk from Hotel Plaza, while Carlton and I waited for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport.” (from left to right: Carlton, Paolo).

Recent findings suggest that the next morning, I found time to talk with Simon Charette and return to the idea of an ORM compatibility matrix. I had started a prototype with Jacob Walls in Palafrugell, and a year later I was talking with someone again to work out what to do next. Simon stressed the value of generating a matrix from the database feature flags automatically and suggested using Python’s descriptor protocol. I already knew how valuable his feedback could be, and it helped to ask him a question, explain what I was unsure about and think it through together.Around us, other people were working on the ORM, the frontend, documentation, Django’s async support and plenty of other topics. People with different levels of experience sat at the same tables, where they could ask for help or a review. I enjoyed seeing people who had been listening at first grow more confident and join the discussions.The mid-morning coffee break, funded by Cuttlesoft, was in the next room. We could get up, have as many freshly made espressos or cappuccinos as we wanted, get something good to eat and talk without having to find a café for the whole group. We had first thought about having breakfast together, but the coffee break fit the rhythm of the three mornings well.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “The coffee break at Hotel Plaza on 25 September, the last morning of sprints.”One thing I never managed to do in Pescara in 2026 was share photos, moments and impressions on my personal Mastodon account as the days went by, as I normally do. This time I took as many photos as I could and wrote a few posts on the official Django on the Med account. Unfortunately, I never got around to posting about the event on my own account, during the sprints or afterwards.Alessia joined us on the second day to take photos and record video. I had asked her to help because I had been free to document everything in Palafrugell, and in Pescara I knew I would have other things to look after. That morning we recorded a few short video interviews with people who wanted to share where they came from and why they were there. I was glad we could keep a record of people’s voices and faces as well as having the photos.The Costa dei TrabocchiFor the afternoon of the first day, I had suggested a bike ride along the Costa dei Trabocchi coast. After lunch on the beach, we walked to the station together to take the regional train to the coastal village of San Vito Chietino. I thought we had plenty of time, but settling the last bills at the restaurant and waiting at the ticket machines meant we still had to hurry a little.«[…] Django on the Med was my first face-to-face encounter with other Djangonauts. I knew the Django community is a friendly place, but my expectations were exceeded.[…] During the lovely afternoon excursions, we were able to connect […] I feel truly inspired and empowered […] I hope that other contributors can have a similar experience […]»As soon as we arrived, everyone bought a return ticket. We had just seen how much a simple thing like that could slow us down, and the small station had only one ticket machine.We picked up the bikes from the shop I had contacted well in advance and headed towards the Adriatic cycle path. Caleb had brought his Brompton folding bike and Johannes his Penny skateboard. The shop owner had warned me that two old rails still crossed the entrance to the path. We needed to get off our bikes to cross them because the wheels could slip. I stopped there to remind the group and wait for the last few people to cross them.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Heading south along the Adriatic cycle path on the Costa dei Trabocchi on the afternoon of 23 September.” (from left to right: Annabelle, Juliana, Jacob W., Jekaterina, Alex, Charlie, Sam)When I looked up, nearly everyone was already cycling the wrong way. Daniele and Valentino had set off north at full speed, and the others had followed, assuming they knew where to go. Caleb went after them and brought them back. I had tried to think of all the little things that might go wrong. The whole group riding away in the opposite direction had never crossed my mind.Once we were all together again, we headed south along the old railway route. The path goes through tunnels and stretches that open out onto the sea. We could ride next to each other, talk and move between groups as we went. I stopped to explain what the trabocchi are and how these wooden fishing structures gave the whole coast its name.On the Punta Isolata headland, part of the group stopped at the kiosk opposite the trabocco of the same name. A few of us went a little further. We took off our shoes on the beach and put our feet in the water. The sea was a little rough, but the summer sun had kept it pleasantly warm. Some people took photos, others watched the waves, and some preferred a drink at the kiosk. I got to show people places I knew well and enjoy them with the people I usually talk to about Django.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “With Annabelle, Sam and Juliana at the water’s edge at Punta Isolata on the afternoon of 23 September.” (from left to right: Paolo, Annabelle, Juliana, Sam)On the way back I reminded everyone that they could make their own plans for dinner. While we waited on the platform at San Vito station, we started forming groups based on what we felt like eating: vegetarian or vegan food, meat or fish. I joined the group that wanted fish. We found a small room for nine at La Barcaccia, a very good seafood restaurant in central Pescara, where we ate local fish and seafood dishes. After spending the afternoon together, it worked well to carry on the evening in smaller groups.«Django on the Med in Pescara was truly amazing. Being able to interact with Django first line contributors in person was truly an unreal experience.I’ve been working remotely for more than 13 years and it still amazes me how refreshing can be to meet fellow developers in person. In the context of advancing a web framework like Django, these events acquire more value than any other means of interaction.»From the hills to the mountainsFor the afternoon of the second day, I also wanted to show people the countryside inland from Pescara. I had thought of several possibilities before I knew which ones we could actually arrange. Mark Smith helped me start the conversation with JetBrains, where he works, and their support made it possible for us to travel together.
