It has been revealed that after the tasting we stopped in Penne, a medieval town on a high hill in the province of Pescara. Vanda, the local guide I had hired at the last minute, took us through Penne’s narrow streets, churches and viewpoints and told us about them in English. I already knew the town, and I still learned something new about its history.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “During the guided walk through the medieval streets of Penne on 24 September.”We stopped for a photo at the highest point in town, about 450 metres above sea level. We could see across the valley and the countryside below, all the way to the sea. Behind the town, the sun was setting over the Gran Sasso massif.Then we headed up towards Villa Celiera, a small mountain village on the slopes of Gran Sasso. We had dinner at La Locanda del Voltigno in a tiny hamlet just over 900 metres above sea level. We were in the Voltigno plateau area, inside the Gran Sasso and Monti della Laga National Park. Cristiano, the owner and chef, had set up a long table in a room for our group. We had worked together on alternatives for the menu, including ciabotto, a traditional vegetable dish. After the first courses, we went outside to see how arrosticini, the local meat skewers, are cooked. Cristiano explained how to cook them on the furnacella, a long, narrow charcoal grill shaped like a gutter, and I translated.Standing around the furnacella, talking and eating the arrosticini while they were still hot from the charcoal was one of the most authentic experiences I could share. I was glad to do it with friends who had come from so many different places. Eventually we went back inside, finished dinner and stayed to talk longer than planned, thanks to a patient bus driver.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the evening of 24 September at La Locanda del Voltigno in Villa Celiera, Cristiano showed us how to cook arrosticini over charcoal, and I translated for the group.” (from left to right: Lilian, Justin, Mark, Jacob R., Paolo, Cristiano)It was already late when we got back on the bus. The lightning over the sea and the conversations that were still going on brought a very full day to a close. With JetBrains’ support, I had been able to arrange much more than the three mornings of sprints.«It’s three serious days. We got 25 people together, and we work solidly in the mornings. The afternoons are more social, and you do carry on the discussions: on the bus coming back from the restaurants, there are people talking about nested transactions and all sorts of things going on.»I was pleased, and I also knew how much work it had taken. I had set aside a week off for the event and kept dealing with bookings, attendance, menus and calls until the last moment. With a room in central Pescara, people could have made their own plans for most afternoons and dinners. I chose to arrange those extra activities because I wanted to share as much of the area as I could with people who had come from far away. When I look back on this edition, I want to remember both what we got from it and how much time it took to make it happen.The last photoOn the last morning, 25 September, the seafront was still wet from the storm we had seen coming in from the sea on the bus ride back. I went for another walk before heading to the hotel. After the long day and a night of rain, some people took the chance to sleep a little longer, so the morning started more slowly. Andrew Northall joined us for the last morning, the only one he could attend.By then people knew their way around the city. They knew the room, had tasks in progress and had sat next to different people over the three days. I had a chance to talk with Carlton about how things had gone and what to do next with Django on the Med.«[…] I’m here because, besides the fun that I’m having here, being in the sprints for a few days charges me and inspires my work on Django for months to come.»We started thinking about the next edition on Gozo, a Maltese island in the middle of the Mediterranean, with help from Joseph Zammit on the island. Joseph had planned everything for Pescara, but had to pull out shortly before the sprints began. Sitting together with Carlton again made that conversation quick too. The year before, we had taken a photo in Dublin to remember our decision to try organizing the first event. This time we took another while Carlton wrote down ideas for the next edition in his notebook.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “On the morning of 25 September, Carlton and I had just decided to meet again on Gozo, Malta, in October 2027 for the third edition of Django on the Med.” (from left to right: Paolo, Carlton)That morning, with the activities from the previous days behind me, I finally got to sit down at my computer. I opened a proposal for an ORM compatibility matrix based on feature flags to write down and share the work from 2025 and the ideas Simon and I had discussed the day before.We stopped at one in the afternoon, half an hour earlier than on the previous days, to give people time to catch their trains or flights without rushing. We took the Django banner outside for our usual group photo in front of the hotel. Then came the goodbyes and hugs. Those who had time stayed for one last quick lunch at Pesciolillo, a small restaurant in the city centre. We tried to make sure the people leaving soon could order first. The station and everyone’s accommodation were close by, so we could spend a bit more time together and everyone could still get on their way easily.After the night of rain, the sun came back quickly and it was even warmer. We ate outside, then those of us still around went for a gelato. By then I was exhausted and very pleased with how it had gone.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Our usual gelato selfie after lunch on Friday 25 September, the last moment together before we said goodbye.” (from left to right: Paolo, Mark, Becky, Jekaterina, Jacob W., Charlie, Sam, Jacob R., Andrew)On each of the three mornings I had managed to do at least one of the things I had planned for the sprints. I talked with Daniele about documentation and AI, discussed the ORM feature flags matrix with Simon and planned the next edition with Carlton. As an organizer, I often felt there was one more thing to do before I could stop. Looking back, I got a lot out of those short conversations too.«Django on the Med 2026 in Pescara is a wrap! 🇮🇹We filed problems, opened pull requests, reviewed code, cycled many kilometres, explored Abruzzo, ate a lot of good food and shared quite a few glasses of wine. We worked, talked, laughed and had a great time together. 🍷Here we all are in our final group photo. Thank you to everyone who made these three days special. 🤗See you in Malta in the 2nd week of October 2027, with Joseph Zammit as our local point person! 🇲🇹»When I got home late that afternoon, I collapsed on the sofa. From the messages and photos in the chat, I could see that some people still in Pescara had gone to the beach and others were making plans for dinner together. I was glad they were still enjoying their time together while I could stop thinking about what came next.«Home, sweet home, but I am missing Pescara already and all the exceptional people that made Django on the Med special.Unmeasurable thanks to Paolo Melchiorre and his fellow locals for keeping us always active, heavenly fed, well caffeinated, incredibly happy and slightly tipsy.What a blast ❤️‍🔥»The last photo I have from those days is of me and Carlton near Pescara’s historic railway station, a few metres from the sprint venue. We are laughing while we wait for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport. We still had time to talk a little about how it had gone and say goodbye with a hug. After so many trips to see each other elsewhere, I was glad I had been able to show him a little of home.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “My last photo of the event, a short walk from Hotel Plaza, while Carlton and I waited for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport.” (from left to right: Carlton, Paolo).

The afternoons are more social, and you do carry on the discussions: on the bus coming back from the restaurants, there are people talking about nested transactions and all sorts of things going on.»I was pleased, and I also knew how much work it had taken. Those who had time stayed for one last quick lunch at Pesciolillo, a small restaurant in the city centre. We tried to make sure the people leaving soon could order first. The station and everyone’s accommodation were close by, so we could spend a bit more time together and everyone could still get on their way easily.After the night of rain, the sun came back swiftly and it was even warmer. We ate outside, then those of us still around went for a gelato. By then I was exhausted and very pleased with how it had gone.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “Our usual gelato selfie after lunch on Friday 25 September, the last moment together before we mentioned goodbye.” (from left to right: Paolo, Mark, Becky, Jekaterina, Jacob W., Charlie, Sam, Jacob R., Andrew)On each of the three mornings I had managed to do at least one of the things I had planned for the sprints. I talked with Daniele about documentation and AI, discussed the ORM feature flags matrix with Simon and planned the next edition with Carlton. As an organizer, I often felt there was one more thing to do before I could stop. Looking back, I got a lot out of those short conversations too.«Django on the Med 2026 in Pescara is a wrap! 🇮🇹We filed problems, opened pull requests, reviewed code, cycled many kilometres, explored Abruzzo, ate a lot of good food and shared quite a few glasses of wine. We worked, talked, laughed and had a great time together. 🍷Here we all are in our final group photo. Thank you to everyone who made these three days special. 🤗See you in Malta in the 2nd week of October 2027, with Joseph Zammit as our local point person! 🇲🇹»When I got home late that afternoon, I collapsed on the sofa. From the messages and photos in the chat, I could see that some people still in Pescara had gone to the beach and others were making plans for dinner together. I was glad they were still enjoying their time together while I could stop thinking about what came next.«Home, sweet home, but I am missing Pescara already and all the exceptional people that made Django on the Med special.Unmeasurable thanks to Paolo Melchiorre and his fellow locals for keeping us always active, heavenly fed, well caffeinated, incredibly happy and slightly tipsy.What a blast ❤️‍🔥»The last photo I have from those days is of me and Carlton near Pescara’s historic railway station, a few metres from the sprint venue. We are laughing while we wait for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport. We still had time to talk a little about how it had gone and say goodbye with a hug. After so many trips to see each other elsewhere, I was glad I had been able to show him a little of home.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “My last photo of the event, a short walk from Hotel Plaza, while Carlton and I waited for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport.” (from left to right: Carlton, Paolo).

I was pleased, and I also knew how much work it had taken. I had set aside a week off for the event and kept dealing with bookings, attendance, menus and calls until the last moment. With a room in central Pescara, people could have made their own intends for most afternoons and dinners. I chose to arrange those extra activities because I wanted to share as much of the area as I could with people who had come from far away. When I look back on this edition, I want to remember both what we got from it and how much time it took to make it happen.The last photoOn the last morning, 25 September, the seafront was still wet from the storm we had seen coming in from the sea on the bus ride back. I went for another walk before heading to the hotel. After the long day and a night of rain, some people took the chance to sleep a little longer, so the morning started more slowly. Andrew Northall joined us for the last morning, the only one he could attend.By then people knew their way around the city. They knew the room, had tasks in progress and had sat next to different people over the three days. I had a chance to talk with Carlton about how things had gone and what to do next with Django on the Med.«[…] I’m here because, besides the fun that I’m having here, being in the sprints for a few days charges me and inspires my work on Django for months to come.»We started thinking about the next edition on Gozo, a Maltese island in the middle of the Mediterranean, with help from Joseph Zammit on the island. Joseph had planned everything for Pescara, but had to pull out shortly before the sprints began. Sitting together with Carlton again made that conversation quick too. From the messages and photos in the chat, I could see that some people still in Pescara had gone to the beach and others were making intends for dinner together. I was glad they were still enjoying their time together while I could stop thinking about what came next.«Home, sweet home, but I am missing Pescara already and all the exceptional people that made Django on the Med special.Unmeasurable thanks to Paolo Melchiorre and his fellow locals for keeping us always active, heavenly fed, well caffeinated, incredibly happy and slightly tipsy.What a blast ❤️‍🔥»The last photo I have from those days is of me and Carlton near Pescara’s historic railway station, a few metres from the sprint venue. We are laughing while we wait for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport. We still had time to talk a little about how it had gone and say goodbye with a hug. After so many trips to see each other elsewhere, I was glad I had been able to show him a little of home.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “My last photo of the event, a short walk from Hotel Plaza, while Carlton and I waited for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport.” (from left to right: Carlton, Paolo).

When I got home late that afternoon, I collapsed on the sofa. From the messages and photos in the chat, I could see that some people still in Pescara had gone to the beach and others were making aims for dinner together. I was glad they were still enjoying their time together while I could stop thinking about what came next.«Home, sweet home, but I am missing Pescara already and all the exceptional people that made Django on the Med special.Unmeasurable thanks to Paolo Melchiorre and his fellow locals for keeping us always active, heavenly fed, well caffeinated, incredibly happy and slightly tipsy.What a blast ❤️‍🔥»The last photo I have from those days is of me and Carlton near Pescara’s historic railway station, a few metres from the sprint venue. We are laughing while we wait for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport. We still had time to talk a little about how it had gone and say goodbye with a hug. After so many trips to see each other elsewhere, I was glad I had been able to show him a little of home.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “My last photo of the event, a short walk from Hotel Plaza, while Carlton and I waited for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport.” (from left to right: Carlton, Paolo).

According to recent reports, the last photo I have from those days is of me and Carlton near Pescara’s historic railway station, a few metres from the sprint venue. We are laughing while we wait for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport. We still had time to talk a little about how it had gone and say goodbye with a hug. After so many trips to see each other elsewhere, I was glad I had been able to show him a little of home.© 2026 Paolo Melchiorre CC BY-SA “My last photo of the event, a short walk from Hotel Plaza, while Carlton and I waited for my wife to pick us up and drive us to the airport.” (from left to right: Carlton, Paolo).
